mr president, commissioners, colleagues, the sale of dangerous products such as untested medicines, counterfeit products, but also the dissemination of illegal content such as that which infringes intellectual property rights, terrorist content or illegal hate speech is a reality that we face every day on the internet. despite this, online sales have been steadily increasing in recent years, and in 2020, at the height of the pandemic, 71% of consumers shopped online. at the same time, it is estimated that 7% of european imports are wasted on counterfeit goods. in such circumstances, we need to increase the level of consumer safety by establishing clear rules on the removal of illegal content from online platforms. the digital services act will ensure a high level of protection for all european consumers, the highest in the world, and prevent the arbitrariness of large multinational companies. consumers often complain that when they buy through an intermediary like ebay or amazon, they don't know exactly who they're buying from. this legislation requires intermediaries to thoroughly vet traders before offering their products and services. furthermore, in addition to raising the level of consumer protection, we want to enable businesses to find new customers online, but not at the expense of the safety of minors. that is why we have prohibited targeted advertising to this category of consumers. finally, i would like to point out that the dissemination of misinformation, especially about vaccination, has unfortunately become a weapon in the hands of irresponsible and malicious people. the spread of fakenews endangers the lives and health of people and this act creates a legal mechanism to combat such behavior. on the other hand, the possibility of censoring content must not be left in any way and in this sense this act represents the right balance. in the age of ubiquitous digitization, it's time to make what's illegal outside the internet illegal on the internet with new rules on digital services.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the widespread digitalisation of business and social life is certainly opening up new possibilities, but it is also opening up a whole range of social and legal issues that have been largely ignored until now. how to regulate digital services, platform abuse, illegal content, algorithms that recommend content or targeted advertising? the priority of the digital transformation must be the protection of the rights of european citizens and consumers, as well as legal certainty. the digital services act is one of the key instruments in this context. we are facing the biggest attempt to modernise european policy in the digital world. the network should thus be more secure and the platforms operating in the common market should have greater legal certainty. the regulation makes it more effective to remove illegal content on networks and to prevent the spread of harmful content, but without censorship, which will ensure a better user experience and the protection of the most vulnerable. personalised advertising, which is important for thousands of small and medium-sized enterprises in their day-to-day business, will not be banned but reinforced by additional transparency requirements. it is equally important that we have exemptions for micro and small enterprises because this is a way to ease the regulatory and administrative burden on those who do not pose a serious risk to the security of the digital world. it's clear that these rules will not solve all the challenges that the new digital age poses to us but it's also clear that we are sending a clear message that everything that is illegal in the analog world is also illegal (interruption).... not for film thin collegiate said heart rate's a few miles a minute. dear chairman, commissioner, colleagues, colleagues, we have recently witnessed the protests that erupted in kazakhstan after the government significantly increased gas prices earlier this year. much is still unclear, but we do know that the protests began in the western part of the country and that the discontent of the citizens soon spread to the rest of the country and grew into a general protest against corruption, poverty and inequality. the demonstrations then escalated into riots and armed clashes, and a number of human rights violations were reported. until these protests, oil-rich kazakhstan, one of the world's largest countries, was considered by many to be the most stable country in a volatile region. of the five central asian countries that gained independence after the collapse of the soviet union, kazakhstan is by far the largest and richest. it is the size of western europe and is situated on vast reserves of oil, natural gas, precious metals, and is the world's largest producer of uranium. the escalation of discontent and the resulting crisis have drawn particular attention from kazakhstan's two powerful neighbors, russia and china. the country places most of its oil exports in china and is traditionally firmly in russia's geopolitical sphere of interest. after days of protests and near-war on the streets of many cities in kazakhstan, the regime has slowly regained control with the help of the collective security treaty organization, which sent in military forces made up mostly of the russian military. russia has thus shown itself to be the strongest partner of the current government in preventing the outbreak of any revolution that could threaten the current government. consequently, the current or any future government in that country will become even more dependent on the kremlin. this shows that moscow still considers the former soviet union as its own backyard. we must be aware of this and not underestimate its power.
thank you, vice president and vice president jourová, secretary of state, colleagues and colleagues. in poland, another law is being debated these days, one that will have negative consequences for the exercise of sexual and reproductive rights, and which aims to deny access to sex education and training. on the contrary, there are brave polish women who, despite intimidation, violence and harassment, managed to collect more than 100,000 signatures for an initiative that would allow abortion up to 12 weeks. unfortunately, poland is not the only country to be troubled by this issue. there's malta, slovakia, hungary, and some other members. while she is not here at the moment, i take this opportunity to remind our president, regardless of all that she stood for before she was elected to this high office, that it remains her duty to defend every document that has come out of this house. it is of the utmost importance that it clearly and loudly defends those who are dealing with sexual and reproductive health because it is a fundamental human right and because denying access to health services in this area is gender-based violence. president macron was very clear and direct about this yesterday and we will follow with great attention his proposal to add the right to safe abortion to the charter of fundamental human rights.
president of the council. mr president, mr pîslaru and your colleagues from the employment committee, i would like to thank you for your commitment and your enthusiastic advocacy, which in some respects also concerns us about what we owe to our young people in europe. we hope this will be all the more so as we emerge from a difficult period of health crisis with its economic and social consequences, which, as we know, have hit europe's young people more than other generations. we have a kind of debt to these young people. we obviously have a number of european policies that existed before the crisis, which are powerful with regard to young people. i will recall a few points. first, and this is more than a symbol, we have collectively adopted the decision on the year 2022 as the european year of youth. we therefore recognize, as you said, an opportunity, but above all a duty, which we have towards our young people. and we have, since the porto summit, under the portuguese presidency of the council of the european union, at the level of the heads of state and government themselves, renewed this european commitment to move forward together with all the institutions, the social partners, in the concrete implementation also of the european framework of social rights which concerns them in particular. in this context, we have collectively supported ambitious targets in an action plan, including further reducing the proportion of young people aged 15-29 who are neither employed nor in education or training - known as neets - from 12.6% to 9%. we have said and shared that youth employment was a priority area before the crisis, and it is even more so after the crisis. and i believe that the whole package of emergency and support measures we have taken during the crisis in all the member states at european level has helped to combat the peak in unemployment. we have not reached the levels we reached during the previous financial or economic crisis, but i want to say even more strongly - because you have mentioned it and i believe that is the deep meaning of your questions - that we must not make youth policy a sectoral policy, an isolated policy. we have these social rights, we have the european year of youth, we have the mobility programmes which specifically concern them. we are celebrating 35 years of the erasmus programme this year. but we must make youth a cross-cutting issue in our european action. when we set up the recovery plan, we also do so for future generations - it is called the 'next european generation'. when we think about our investment policy and the budgetary policy of tomorrow, we also think about young people. when we think about new initiatives targeting young people, but which are very broad - i am thinking of european civic service, which the french presidency intends to build on existing european programmes in conjunction with the european commission - and when the european commission and president von der leyen propose the alma programme, which aims to offer opportunities to young people who do not yet have them, particularly in terms of mobility, i believe that on all these issues we are making a collective effort and that we are making progress. i know that your committee is particularly committed to the issue of platform workers, and it is often to these young workers who are starting their working lives, sometimes in difficult conditions, that we also turn. finally, i would like to say that the conference on the future of europe, which will be held here in a few hours' time and in which i will be participating, also offers a future perspective which concerns our young people first and foremost. if we want to reform our institutions, our policies, our priorities, our budgets, our actions, to make them more effective, we do it also and above all for the young. this youth policy has multiple inputs, but it is ultimately our common promise that we make through all the great actions that we have taken collectively, in the face of the crisis in particular, and to prepare this world after that which we are building today.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, it’s my great honour to address this house today to talk about our youth and how to empower them. i express my thanks for the very relevant questions that have been put to me today. as president von der leyen said in her state of the union speech last september, our youth is our most educated, talented and motivated generation, and europe needs all of its youth. it is our duty to do everything in our power to protect their future. this is why this year is the european year of youth. it is dedicated to empowering our young people and setting a legacy for future generations beyond 2022. it is being co-created with young people. the european youth portal is one of the key hubs to gather ideas and initiatives throughout the whole year of 2022. when it comes to employment and social policies, we are not starting from scratch. the reinforced youth guarantee commits member states to ensure that all young people under the age of 30 receive a good quality offer of employment, continued education, apprenticeship or traineeship within a period of four months of becoming unemployed or leaving education. this recommendation of the commission was adopted by the council in record time in 2020 and received a warm welcome by this house, which held a debate and adopted a resolution with a huge majority in october 2020. last year, in october, the council of the eu employment committee (emco) reviewed work so far in member states. challenges remain in many areas, but there is a more systematic approach in tackling youth unemployment and inactivity. the union is doing its part. the new esf+ regulation requires that member states with a neet rate above the eu average allocate at least 12.5 % of their national allocation to youth employment measures. furthermore, the commission is setting up alma (aim, learn, master, achieve). alma is based on other successful initiatives financed by the european social fund in member states. it is proof that we do not leave anyone behind, as alma is a cross-border youth mobility scheme for the most disadvantaged young people aged 18 to 30, those who are not in employment, education or training and who are most vulnerable with regard to their chances of accessing work or training for either individual or structural reasons. our young people need education and training systems that are adapted to the societies of today and tomorrow. green, digital and entrepreneurial skills must be the cornerstone of our approach. in 2020, member states agreed on an ambitious recommendation to modernise and improve the vocational educational system. this will be crucial for young people to get their feet into the labour market, in particular through apprenticeship. there are also other ambitious initiatives in the skills domain, such as individual learning accounts and micro—credentials that are innovative approaches that will be of benefit for young people. thanks to the european skills agenda and the pact for skills, since november 2020 six large—scale partnerships have been established in the sectors of automotive, microelectronics, aerospace and defence, offshore renewable energy, shipbuilding and maritime technology, textiles, clothing, leather and footwear. over 500 organisations have pledged to create a culture of learning in their workplaces, including via apprenticeships. together, these alliances will support 1.5 million people. traineeships can also be helpful instruments for young people, provided they meet certain minimum quality requirements, which we are currently reviewing. last, but certainly not least, we need to address mental health as a priority and stop the stigma around it. young people, in particular, have paid a very high price during the pandemic. this is an area where the union has limited ability to act, but let me point out that cohesion policy funds do allow member states to invest in ensuring equal access to healthcare, fostering the resilience of health systems and promoting the transition from institutional to family and community-based care. the instruments and resources are there, and so is the political will. i know i can count fully on the european parliament’s contribution to ensure that the european year of youth will be a success and empower our younger generation. just to repeat what clément beaune said about the conference on the future of europe, for a very good reason we devoted a lot of space and we encouraged a lot of young people to contribute in the very broad debate on how they see the future of europe. the conference is starting tomorrow, 21 january 2022, with the working groups and the plenary. i’m very much looking forward to meeting the young people here because this is a new wave of very fresh air.
on behalf of the renew group. – mr president, we start 2022, the european year of youth, still in the grip of a pandemic. since the beginning, the virus and the effect of the health measures have greatly affected our youth’s lives and their well-being. the pandemic has taken a toll on mental health, with the number of young europeans suffering from anxiety and depression rising steeply; it has had an impact on their social lives and on their education, with school closures and shifting to online learning, and it has only worsened their employment prospects. madam commissioner, we must put our youth needs first in this post-pandemic recovery. we must do everything to empower and invest in our youth, to encourage their creativity, to innovate and for them to be ready to help to solve the challenges of our times, such as the digital and green transitions.
on behalf of the left group. – mr president, i welcome this question from the committee on employment and social affairs. our young people have suffered hugely during covid. they have sacrificed years of their young lives to keep others safe. decent pay, a living wage and properly resourced mental health services are important to many young people and must be championed in a post-covid world and immediately. specific policies for youth are all well and good, but we must look at the type of society young people are growing up in and entering as adults. unfortunately, in an irish context, that society is deeply unfair. this is down to irish government policy. the irish state refuses to use its resources to solve issues like childcare and health issues. instead, it farms out as much as it can to private actors. this is especially damaging in housing. where their parents could afford a family home on a single average wage, younger couples can now both be working in well-paid jobs and still not be able to afford a home for their young family, stuck renting at sky-high prices, and that’s before childcare costs. the advocating of vulture funds and similar financial vehicles means that young families and single young people simply cannot compete with billion-dollar funds buying up what little homes there are. it is deeply unfair. young people deserve a society where a home is affordable. that is something that should – and can – be delivered on with the right policies.
mr. president, i have to ask you... it's not big words that young people need, but good help, knowledge, a home of their own and, say, financial support. in our country, starting this year, young people under 25 will not have to pay personal income tax, and they will be able to take home more money up to the average income. this measure affects 280 000 young people already in employment. there's also an interest-free student loan that young women who have children later, who have more children, don't have to pay back. it also helps to start a family. these measures are also confirmed by eurostat data. the risk of poverty among young people has decreased from 2.4% to 12% despite the coronavirus pandemic, and youth unemployment has decreased from 10% to 8.7% in ten years. i would recommend that the other eu member states follow our example and use the resources of the european union in this way.
commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is now quite clear that the coronavirus pandemic will leave many negative consequences, unfortunately, and one of the most affected groups will certainly be our young people. in 2022, the european year of youth. i'm not going to talk about their concerns about entertainment, social life, education, of course, social inclusion, but i'm going to talk about the unemployment problem. i think that the commission, together with the member states, has a huge responsibility here. all of this, of course, has to be seen in the light of the demographic problems of europe as a whole and particularly of my country of origin, croatia. unemployment directly affects young people's mental health, creates fear, insecurity and defines young people's behaviour and, of course, determines their thinking about future plans and family formation. ladies and gentlemen, i believe that after two years, we are finally coming to the end of this unfortunate pandemic, but this is not a time for celebration, but for the urgent preparation of post-pandemic relief measures and concrete programs. i ask you therefore to give top priority to young people who have been very deprived and suffering for almost two years and who urgently need concrete measures and programmes to make life and work easier and try to return them and the whole society to the old, i would say only normal, as soon as possible. we need a common european response because just as covid knows no borders, the response to covid must be borderless, be european, a common response and in this way we will help young people but also our entire society to recover faster. so let's look at investing in young people as an important investment, not as a cost.
madam president, the attitude towards children and young people in europe that we have seen and are seeing in the wake of the covid crisis is terrible, as if we were watching which country will inflict more harm on its youngest population with bad policies, as if it were some kind of competition. unfortunately, the damage done is incalculable. locking up in homes and apartments, dropping out of school, massive losses of hours and materials in education, a campaign of fear and panic by the media and the government, mandatory mask-wearing in schools, conditional passes to enter educational institutions themselves, are already announcing lasting psychological consequences. there's an increased suicide rate in this age group. there's a push for vaccination even though the chance of side effects is much greater than the risk from covid, which is almost unmeasurable for this population. pharmacovigilance systems show a dramatic spike in myocarditis in children and adolescents as a side effect of vaccination. all this was happening with almost no justification in science or practice. therefore, the post-pandemic and social recovery that we are talking about will not be possible until those who participated in this terror are held accountable.
ladies and gentlemen, if we fail to act decisively, cancer will increase by more than 24% by 2035 and become the leading cause of death in the european union. the time is ripe for radical and comprehensive action, and the adoption of the european plan to fight cancer and this report are key steps in that direction. this report contains a number of specific measures which i myself have proposed. it calls for a single set of rules on reimbursement of cross-border healthcare, including the right to a second opinion, to simplify and facilitate access to treatment and clinical trials, in particular for patients suffering from rare cancers and for children, whose day we are marking today. it is particularly important that costs are funded before treatment starts in order to avoid discrimination against low-income patients, thus demonstrating that solidarity is our priority. in addition, we stress the importance of increased investment in health infrastructure and health workers through cohesion policy in order to reduce disparities in the quality of care across the eu and to provide equal access to quality healthcare for all our citizens. the aim is to ensure that the union's citizens have access to medicines, regardless of where they live, by collectively procuring high-priced medicines. we are sending a clear message: there can be no first and second class citizens in the european union. finally, we are calling for european legislation to guarantee the right to be forgotten for cancer survivors. no one will be discriminated against or have difficulty accessing financial services because they have overcome this disease. the report before us represents a huge step forward in the fight against cancer, especially for patients and their families, and i ask you to support it by voting for it.

on behalf of the renew group. – mr president, corruption is the economic, political and psychological aspect of human rights violations. it distorts socio—economic realities, hinders access to public goods and quality services, and creates artificial hierarchies that impose additional costs on citizens and entire societies. the eu needs real magnitsky sanctions that include corruption as a sanctionable offence. our democratic allies – the united states, canada, united kingdom and australia – all include corruption under their magnitsky legislations. the absence of corruption at the eu level is not only restrictive but also means that we lag behind our global partners. by avoiding to punish high—level corruption, thus we become accomplices to human rights crimes. we fail the same people – human rights defenders and democracy activists – who we unconditionally support. corrupt autocrats repress peaceful protests, illegally imprison people demanding basic freedoms and rights, and despite all of this, are free to use their ill—gotten wealth to maintain their power and enjoy a life of luxury, including real estate, holidays and education of their children in the west. together with colleagues, we have repeatedly called on the european external action service and the council to put an end to such abuses, and to update the european union magnitsky act to include corruption. we will insist until this done.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, there are a growing number of authoritarian regimes run by kleptocrats and extractive elites. they blatantly use power to enrich themselves, while repressing dissenting voices to ensure that they can continue to steal with impunity. against this background, the eu needs to lead global efforts to end systemic corruption, which erodes human rights, institutionalises crime and perpetuates inequalities. we should take full advantage of our foreign policy toolbox in order to tackle big corruption. but corruption is a complex and global phenomenon that, in one way or another, affects all countries and therefore requires efforts both at international level and at home. in this regard, the eu needs to be more effective in combating, within its borders, issues like tax evasion, illicit trade, banking secrecy and money laundering, shortcomings in the implementation by member states of eu anti-corruption regulations, eu—based secrecy jurisdictions and tax havens, and certain financial and legal actors based in the eu. all this emboldens corrupt actors outside the eu and allows the disguising of illicit financial flows into the eu. therefore, our report includes a call to recognise the responsibility of certain eu—based individuals and entities as enablers or beneficiaries of corruption abroad and to take action. it is time for the eu to push back against the global threat of kleptocracy, and this also requires acting against complicity happening right before our eyes.

the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of toys is not compromised by the fact that they are not safe for children. this is evidenced by the fact that, according to the latest information from the register of dangerous non-food products, toys were the most frequently notified product, with as much as 27% of all notifications. we therefore need to improve the safety rules for children's toys, especially those imported into europe from third countries. the internet is the place where dishonest merchants often offer unsafe toys to consumers. the digital services act, which parliament is involved in, will provide rules to ensure secure shopping in the digital environment. in addition, the ageing of chemical use should be removed as part of the review of the toy safety directive. they are concerned, in particular, by reports that some manufacturers deliberately misrepresent that a toy is intended for children over 36 months of age in order to avoid compliance with the stricter safety standards for children under 36 months of age. it is therefore important that the european commission investigates in detail such allegations and market practices as soon as possible and, in line with the findings, proposes amendments to the relevant legislation. when it comes to the safety of our children, we have never, and will never, and must never, compromise!

dear chair, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the uncertainty of the pandemic, the pandemic lockdowns, online education, the declining prospects for employment, for self-employment, but also for starting a family, have affected our generation, the generation of young people, in the last two years in a way that has rarely affected any generation in the last decades. that is why all the policies and programmes we decide on here in the european parliament must prevent the opening up and creation of new and lost generations and must send a clear message that the recovery of europe without young people is simply impossible. digital transformation is dramatically changing the way that we all, and especially young people, communicate, do business and live, and that is certainly a challenge, but it is also an opportunity. i've seen this through my travels to various successful entrepreneurial stories, but also to different parts of croatia, places like lipik and novska. because digital transformation is an opportunity, an opportunity to offer a new way of learning, an opportunity for the quality jobs of the future. the innovation and creativity of young people must be brought to the fore by removing administrative barriers and opening up space for young people across europe to shape their future independently and with a sense of urgency future in all parts of the european union.

the next item is the major interpellation for written answer with debate by mara bizzotto to the commission on strengthening the system for protecting pdo and pgi denominations in the eu after the prosecco/prosek case (g—001003/2021) (b9-0004/2022). i would like to remind members that for all the debates of this part-session there will be no catch—the—eye procedure and no blue cards will be accepted. furthermore, as during recent part-sessions, remote interventions from parliament’s liaison offices in the member states are foreseen. i would also like to remind you that interventions in the chamber will continue to be made from the central rostrum. i therefore kindly invite you to keep an eye on the speakers’ list and to approach the rostrum when your speaking time is imminent.
author. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the croatian prošek is a fraud against our prosecco, against made in italy and against consumers all over the world, and it threatens to destroy the european system of pdo and pgi marks. today, for the first time, the european parliament's plenary session is dealing with the prosecco/prošek war, which has become a symbolic case throughout europe. the european commission has behaved in a scandalous way: in september 2021, it launched the process for the recognition of prošek and since then it has closed in shameful silence, despite the fact that three months have passed since the official position presented by italy. it is equally scandalous that today, officially speaking on behalf of the european commission, there is not the commissioner for agriculture, but another commissioner. what have you got to hide? why does the commissioner for agriculture not have the courage to come to parliament and say clearly what he wants to do about the prošek case? what game are you playing? it is worth recalling that croatia had already tried to obtain recognition of prošek in 2013, but was rejected by then commissioner cioloş. i quote the words of cioloş in reply to my question: 'the use of the term prošek in trade may create legal problems, as the croatian name could conflict with the protection of the italian pdo prosecco. the croatian authorities are aware of this legal problem.' what has changed in these nine years to justify this dramatic turn of events by the commission? today, as then, croatian prošek is in open conflict with the pdo prosecco, which protects the quality of our wine and the work of 8 000 italian producers. recognising the name prošek would be a very serious breach of european regulations, which protect pdo and pgi products from any attempt to evoke, imitate and distort the name, even when the name is translated. the system of protected designations of origin and geographical indications is the cornerstone of the european union's agri-food policy. if europe does not want to defend prosecco, which is the most famous and best-selling italian wine in the world, what will happen to other italian products in europe with pdo and pgi? europe must strengthen the instruments that defend our products, not scrap the system of pdos and pgi using the trojan horse of prošek. the time for small talk is over. europe must say no to the request for recognition of prošek. the only true prosecco is the italian prosecco, produced in veneto and friuli, recognized and protected by the doc and docg designations. europe is playing for its head. we will not give up an inch, and we are ready at the barricades to defend our prosecco and all made in italy.
member of the commission. – madam president, good afternoon honourable members, honourable member of parliament bizzotto. so before addressing the two specific questions raised by this major interpellation, i shall first provide a brief update on the application for protection of the croatian traditional term prošek. as it is customary, the publication for objection of the traditional term prošek was preceded by cautious commission scrutiny based on the applicable legal framework. given that the croatian application fulfilled all the relevant legal conditions, the commission published the application on 22 september last year. all interested parties had two months from the date of publication to submit an objection. the commission received 12 objections. so according to eu rules, admissible objections will be transmitted to croatia for observations. then any observation provided by croatia will be communicated to italy. following these exchanges and on the basis of all available information of eu legislation and of the jurisprudence of the european court of justice, the commission will take the final decision to protect or reject the traditional term prošek through a commission implementing regulation. as regards the reform of the eu geographical indications system, the commission services are currently working on a proposal to strengthen the system of geographical indications, which, as you know, is a major agricultural policy objective of the von der leyen commission. the commission intends to adopt the legislative proposal in april and submit it to the co-legislators. the commission proposal will aim to strengthen the system of geographical indications for all agricultural sectors. it will contribute to the objectives of the common agricultural policy, addressing societal demands on food and health and thus the objectives of the european green deal and farm to fork strategy. the proposal will be underpinned by an impact assessment analysing and assessing options based on facts and evidence, and i can reassure you that the commission is taking the concerns raised in this major interpellation into consideration. while the fundamental structure of the eu gi system is sound, its revision focuses on two general objectives: ensuring effective protection of intellectual property rights within the eu, including efficient registration processes to further reward producers for their efforts, and increasing the uptake of gis across the eu to benefit the rural economy. more specifically, the commission intends to further enhance the protection of protected designations of origin and protected geographical indications, including defining the scope of avocation. in addition to this, the whole system of protection against direct or indirect commercial use, misuse, avocation, imitation, should also apply to terms used in internet domain names, having regard to goods and services presented in the web page under that name. furthermore, the commission also intends to introduce stricter rules on the use of gi names in the sale names of processed goods when the gi product is an ingredient of the processed product. in relation to your second question, the eu has an extensive set of rules on official controls in the agrifood chain to provide efficient and stronger enforcement tools at national level. member states’ competent authorities must control if the producers comply with the rules set forth for geographical indications and take the necessary steps to stop unlawful practices on their territories. a network of food fraud contact points in the member states has been set up to facilitate administrative assistance and cooperation in cases of cross—border violations. the european anti-fraud office is a member of this network. it has launched several inquiries targeting in particular infringement of intellectual property rights, including geographical indications on its own initiative or to support the enforcement activities of competent national agencies against cross-border frauds. the administrative assistance and cooperation system set up by the european commission is providing a confidential network between the competent authorities, enabling member states and, under specific conditions, stakeholders, to notify each other of suspected usurpations of geographical indications and to request action. moreover, to ensure monitoring of the control done by member states, the commission is auditing their official control systems and intervenes in case of systemic failure to apply eu law. the commission also organises training sessions and seminars on control issues with regard to geographical indications in order to provide guidance and share best practices beyond our borders. the eu is fully committed to enhancing the international protection of eu geographical indications by including a comprehensive gi provisions in the intellectual property chapters in bilateral agreements. this way, a comparable level of protection and enforcement of gis is guaranteed in the partner country to that of the eu. each bilateral agreement protecting gis lays down obligations on both parties to verify compliance of the products using these gis with production rules, as well as specific enforcement mechanisms to apply on the party’s respective markets. the commission also engages with trading partners in intellectual property dialogues and intellectual property technical cooperation programmes, which promote the protection and effective enforcement of gis in line with eu standards. in addition, in accordance with regulation no 608/2013 on customs enforcement of intellectual property rights, member states’ customs authorities may act at the border and detain goods suspected of infringing pdos and pgis. regulation 608/2013 also gives olaf a specific mandate for mutual assistance in fighting counterfeit goods imported from third countries. olaf has been active in that field for several years, as illustrated by the dismantling of a fraudulent network producing fake rum. finally, counterfeit food is also the focus of regular cross-border operations between member state law enforcement, such as through the opson operations within the framework of the eu policy cycle on serious and organised crime facilitated by europol with the active participation of olaf.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, imagine a request for a traditional term called 'champagne' or perhaps 'portolese'. does that sound crazy? i sincerely do, too. but it does not seem so absurd, otherwise we would not have had the debate we have here today. prosecco is not just any name in europe. with 600 million bottles it is the most produced sparkling wine in europe and the european union and one of the most important designations of origin, both in numbers and value, known throughout the world. is it serious to jeopardize this denomination with the traditional mention? it's not a good idea. the commission should have blocked this attack on prosecco years ago, partly because croatia, which is already on the verge of joining the european union, has basically accepted that it does not want to claim this name. during a debate in the committee on agriculture a few months ago, the commission representatives told us that there was no danger, because these are very different products. this will be true for the italian market, for the croatian market, perhaps even for consumers and the european market. but are we sure that a consumer in tokyo or new york understands that prosciutto and prosecco are two completely different things? i don't think he did. i realize that the regulation is unclear, perhaps even superficial, when it allows the traditional term to coexist with the designation of origin, and here we must intervene, commissioner. but if we do not accept this, how can we seriously defend the denominations, especially the most well-known ones in the world, with the mechanism you have just highlighted? the question could become a precedent for other important wine and agri-food denominations. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud, and i am sure that the commission will take them seriously. what do we say tomorrow if we ourselves, at home, accept a situation like this? so let's stop this nonsense! it is too dangerous, not only for prosecco and for the denominations as a whole, but i think it is not good for prošek either, since the copy is always worse than the original.

the debate on the differences and similarities between croatian wine and italian prosecco has been going on in the eu institutions since croatia's accession to the european union, and the italian side has systematically obstructed the protection of the authenticity of the prosecco and its positioning on the european wine map and market. the italian claims that the rag is a new product, or a copy of their average, and that there is therefore a risk of misleading consumers and unfair competition are completely incorrect. croatian vesek is a dense and sweet wine that falls into the category of dessert, while italian prosecco is a lightly sparkling wine. since these are two products of different colours, smells and prices, different traditions and consumption habits, the italian side's protest is pointless. there are no points of contact in taste, production method or type of grapes in comparison of these two products, and the vinegar has an invaluable significance in the centuries-old, historical and oenological tradition of croatia. i'm going with "multi-century". i welcome the fact that the commission took a reasonable position in its written observations last year and indicated that homonymy, or similarity of names, is not sufficient to reject the croatian claim and that, especially in view of the local and traditional use, homonyms in this sense can coexist as in the present case. i am also pleased that the commission has recently published the croatian application for protection of the traditional term "žžek" in the official journal of the eu and that the procedure for taking a final decision is ongoing. in view of the above, i can safely say that croatian sparkling wine and italian prosecco do not belong to the same group of wines. croatia has a strong historical and legal legitimacy to recognise the traditional term "pasek" at the european union level. despite italy's arguments and obstruction, croatia, as a full member of the european union, has every right to protect its traditional indigenous product, the latex, and it does, and i am convinced that the commission will take a positive decision for croatia in this case and thus confirm the equality of all before the rules of the common market.
the honourable chairman, the commissioner. thank you to my colleague salima who was the reporter for this documentary, and to all the shadow reporters for a job well done. the problem of racism in the european union, unfortunately, is not a matter of individual incidents but a structural problem woven into almost all pores of society. some of the best channels to address this problem are education, culture, media and sport. education systems across the union must include courses and colleges that address this issue and teach children and young people that racism has no place in our society, but rather that it is a place where our differences come together, where we learn to express ourselves individually, where we create connections that carry those of our core values: openness, equality and tolerance. while racism is unfortunately still present in all these areas, as this report shows, we have a great deal of work to do to defeat it once and for all. and that is why for those who incite, promote and implement racism we demand the strictest punishments, without exception.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the healthcare sector, in particular the healthcare sector in the eu. you're much more likely to die of cancer if you live in the less developed regions of the european union than in the more developed parts of western and northern europe, which is unacceptable. we must do everything we can to eliminate these differences, and the most effective instrument for this is cohesion policy. cohesion policy promotes growth and employment in regions throughout the union, and in particular in those that are lagging behind. about one third of the eu budget is used for this purpose. the cohesion policy is therefore not only the union's most important investment policy, but also an expression of solidarity between it and its member states. the commission should also be able to use the resources of the european regional development fund and the european social fund to support the implementation of the union's regional policy objectives, in particular by supporting the implementation of the union's regional policy objectives. however, inequalities in health between member states remain a reality and cohesion investments in health need to be further increased and used more efficiently than they have been so far. the report identifies the main problems of the current situation and points to potential solutions. the biggest problem of health inequalities is seen in the elements of health infrastructure, but also in the lack of manpower that directly affects the success of treatment in less developed regions. this is why long-term investment in health infrastructure and workforce is more urgent than ever, especially in view of the economic and health consequences of the pandemic. citizens in rural areas, outermost regions and islands often face barriers to equal access to healthcare, which limit their ability to obtain much-needed services. there must be no first-class citizens and no second-class citizens in the european union, and services must be available to all and available in a timely manner. in order to overcome the main obstacles to equal access to healthcare in rural areas, advanced technologies such as ehealth should be used extensively as an integral part of the smart villages concept with the aim of improving access to healthcare. furthermore, we need to motivate health workers, especially doctors and nurses, to start or continue their work in rural areas. the commission shall be empowered to adopt delegated acts in accordance with article 264 concerning the establishment of a european network of experts in the field of intellectual property rights. in addition, the improvement of working conditions will encourage a strategy to retain health workers in these areas. i also called in the report for cohesion policy funds to be used to develop specialised centres of excellence for specific diseases across the eu, reaching out to neighbouring countries and contributing to cross-border cooperation in healthcare. it is necessary to use in synergy all existing eu instruments, such as eu health and horizon europe, to develop a network of such centres spread evenly across the union. the commission has also received a number of complaints from the member states concerning the lack of transparency in the implementation of the union's health policy. the pilot project will also provide a platform for the exchange of information on the implementation of the union's health policy and the health policy of the member states, including the implementation of the union's health policy and the health policy of the member states. finally, it is crucial that when evaluating the impact of cohesion policy, account is taken of how much investment has contributed to improving health outcomes, such as cancer recovery rates, and this is precisely the area where we see the greatest disparities in the european union today. the covid-19 pandemic represents a historic milestone for investments in health systems and i therefore call on member states to seek to mobilise the maximum amount of structural funds to meet the health needs of european citizens in the new programming period.

on behalf of the band the left. mr president, the political situation in bosnia and herzegovina is deteriorating in a unique way in this poor, landlocked and divided country, which has not really overcome the trauma of the civil war and whose institutions are not functioning well. tensions are growing between the so-called croat-muslim federation of bosnia and the serb republic of bosnia. the latter, it has been said, no longer conceals its desire to secede. milorad dodik, the political leader of the bosnian serbs, no longer recognises the legitimacy of sarajevo and demands to be allowed to have its own army, its own judicial system and its own taxation system. in this perilous context, of course, vladimir putin's war throws a blinding, raw light on the plans of mr dodik, who makes no secret of his allegiance to russia and relies on its support to achieve his goals. and then there is the united states, the promoter of the dayton agreements, which is very interested in the region and which has adopted sanctions against mr dodik. we are therefore in the process of internationalising the conflict in the balkan powder keg. and all the evidence suggests that if there is no quick, determined action by the european union, mr commissioner, there could be a second disaster at europe's borders. because of course the ukrainian catastrophe affects us all, but there is a problem of security, of credibility. we must therefore clarify our own position, and that can only be the territorial integrity of bosnia. we must then ascertain serbia's intentions - i saw that the serbian president had encouraged republika srpska to participate in the collegial institutions of bosnia. finally, we must find a way out from above, because europe cannot afford to sit idly by. she can't procrastinate. our safety is at stake.
mr. chairman, i want to share with you here a positive story from bosnia and herzegovina regarding the protection of the human and constitutional rights of citizens. in addition to the fact that more than a year ago the constitutional court of bih declared the obligation to wear masks and the restriction of movement unconstitutional, we also have new decisions. on 23 february this year, the constitutional court of bih made a decision on the appeal of lawyer ajanović declaring unconstitutional the covid certificates and declaring unconstitutional the mandatory vaccination introduced by narrow segments of the executive power. this was decided by the grand chamber of the constitutional court and three international judges. the court even criticized the parliament for allowing the executive to allow the violation of the rule of law and democracy in the country. according to the court's decision, those who disobey this decision should be prosecuted under the criminal law with a sentence of six months to five years. the norwegian code and council of europe resolution 2361 are also accepted as arguments. the constitutional court ordered that the public authorities must immediately comply with the decision and bring their actions into line with the constitution and the european convention on human rights. this is a victory against segregation and discrimination and a testament to what people can do when they come together. i congratulate bosnia and herzegovina on this example of the protection of human and constitutional rights from which all of europe can learn.
mr president, vice-president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the stability of bosnia and herzegovina is essential for the maintenance of peace and security in the region of south-east europe. in order to ensure stability in that state, the priority is to overcome the political crisis in which the croats, as a constituent people, are imposed illegitimate representatives at the level of the presidency of bih and the house of peoples of the parliament of the federation of bih. this is possible, only and exclusively, by a rapid amendment of the electoral law that will ensure the equality of the croatian people in bih, who, colleagues, are denied the political right that is not denied to practically any other people in europe - to choose their political representatives. this right was recognised by a decision of the constitutional court of bih, which, unfortunately, is not applicable. it is therefore essential that electoral reforms ensure legitimate representation in all three constituent peoples in an equal manner, as is the case in belgium, and put an end to the hegemony of the largest people. it is scandalous that some members here in parliament are denying this fundamental right which is also enjoyed by people in belgium, for example, and by people in germany. i will repeat this time, as i have said many times from this platform, the question of protecting the croats in bosnia and herzegovina, who are practically all citizens of the european union, is the question of preserving fundamental european values such as the rule of law, but also the key to the stability of this part of europe.
mr president, commissioner, the war in ukraine has exposed the structural weaknesses of the european union in a sector as critical as energy. for years, instead of moving towards greater independence, we have been moving towards increasing dependence. and now we have the highest prices in the world. gas prices have risen by up to 400% in recent months, and are already a stifling cost to industry, businesses and households. we must act immediately, starting with lowering prices. we have room for manoeuvre by reducing vat, electricity taxes or eliminating special levies. we must immediately, as soon as possible, diversify the sources of supply territorially and develop new interconnection projects for gas and hydrogen. the commission should extend the list of projects of common interest to include the step project between the iberian peninsula and france. in short, it is imperative to build an interconnected europe that allows the free movement of gas and electricity.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the spiral of gas prices is becoming more and more an unbearable emergency for the budgets of middle-class households and for the production of certain industrial sectors, which are in danger of suspending their activities with serious damage to the continental economy and to the productive capacity of our continent. this calls for bold action. the commission is doing this, and you must do it even more, madam commissioner, by addressing the mechanisms of price formation - a very uncomfortable subject but one that must be tackled - which today is anchored to the marginal price of gas by a spiral that, in the end, no longer has any reference to the underlying value, that is, the exchange value of the commodity. we must have the courage to act at european level, that is, at union level, on common stocks and common purchases, if we want to reduce dependence and reduce costs, and we must find resources, because these are not zero-cost interventions and, if the resources are not in the budget, we must find mechanisms such as sure, which have had a good response from the market and have given a little oxygen to the initiative of the member states.
mr president, commissioner simson, ladies and gentlemen, a year ago a megawatt hour of gas cost 17 euros, today it is 295. in the delta between 17 and 295 there is the russian invasion and the crisis that has plunged many of our businesses, which were recovering from the pandemic, and that of many families who do not have money to pay for heating and gas. it is therefore good that the commission has just published a communication setting out common european action to deal with this situation. security of supply, economic and environmental sustainability, energy are the main objectives, especially if we want to become independent from russia and send a clear political message. we need instruments to mitigate the price crisis, we need to prepare ourselves today for the coming winter through storage and common purchasing, we need european infrastructure to enable the sharing and diversification of supply, just as we need to set up new renewable energy plants and an ambitious energy efficiency programme. let's move from good intentions to action and allocate the resources today to be free tomorrow.
mr. president, resources have become a political tool. to prevent manipulation, we must redirect investment as quickly as possible to safeguard the independence of the european union. let's really accelerate the reduction of fossil fuels, uranium, coal. more importantly, we must make a principled decision not to pay for russia's war with our money. let's stop importing gas and oil from russia. your analysis, commission, says that the states can do without russian energy until winter. as you know, today it was announced that the united states and the united kingdom have already decided to abandon russian fossil fuels. today, this step is postponed to protect the economy, to protect the people. but what will we have to protect ourselves against if putin's army starts bombing our cities? let's put the money in the most vulnerable groups. share the infrastructure available in your member states, minimising any negative consequences. together we can do this!
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i think that we should not be lighting candles, and a fundamental problem in connection with the rise in energy prices is probably the architecture of the energy market, which, thanks to liberalisation, simply favours speculation and dubious business models. we must act in the short term, namely by combating energy poverty through social tariffs, free energy quotas and the prohibition of power cuts. but we also need to take a long-term view - rethinking europe's electricity and gas markets and clearly shifting towards renewable energy. that doesn't mean we're replacing one gas with another, as long as it's not russian! this can't be the way! we must also discuss the marginal cost model, for example. this must be examined because it allows large corporations to make huge profits at the expense of consumers. and we have to understand that energy prices are a social problem. what we also need is a right to energy, for all.
mr. chairman, ladies and gentlemen, fellow citizens, the price of energy rarely, if ever, matches the price of its production and distribution. they depend mostly on geopolitics and the wars that are started. we had almost the same gasoline and diesel prices when oil was $120 a barrel and when it was $40 a barrel. wars bring enormous profits to some of the oil industry groups. it happened now. just as fake and real pandemics bring huge profits to the pharmaceutical industry, so wars bring huge profits to the energy industry. it will be so today and it will be so forever because wars are constantly being created and constantly being invented.
mr. chairman, congratulations to our rapporteurs irene and gwendoline for doing a great job, and to all the other colleagues who have contributed to this report. it is no small task to look at all the relevant topics within gender equality and give us a comprehensive overview of our institution's position in this area, while giving a wider social context. what i can stress further, and what is apparent from the report, is that there is a lot of work ahead of us and we cannot be satisfied with the current situation. i particularly emphasise the call for gender-sensitive budgeting. nothing we want to achieve is possible without adequate allocation of resources. we cannot stand here month after month condemning violence against women, railing against the lack of access to decision-making processes, or being angry about the lack of a mechanism to suppress the gender pay gap, without, as one of the basic prerequisites, providing the means to change all of that. we should also not forget the impact of the covid-19 pandemic on gender equality and this should always be emphasised. and it's incredible that, for example, the femm committee's requirement that every national recovery plan must have a chapter on gender equality has not been met. there can be no recovery of society, the economy and the european community without full implementation of the principle of gender equality. and as long as half of our society is considered secondary, and we often hear this said about women, we have no european future.
madam president, we are receiving many accusations from ukraine, including from president zelensky himself, that the west has left them in the lurch. for years, zelensky has heard words of support and encouragement and promises of joining nato and the european union. and now that it's under russian attack, except for some weapons and equipment, the west is doing nothing because it doesn't want to risk world war iii. wasn't it the same with georgia, years of promises of encouragement and support, but when it came to the russian intervention in georgia itself, it was left alone. there are sanctions, of course. the west is prepared to kick russia out of swift, for which they have an alternative, sanction russian cats, vodka, cancel cross-border cooperation of national parks, sanction tchaikovsky and dostoevsky, who have nothing to do with this war. but serious sanctions, oil, gas, nuclear fuel, the west is very very restrained and continues to de facto finance russia. what i have to mention is the latest information that has appeared in both american and chinese media about several dozen bio-laboratories on the territory of ukraine that are under the direct control of the us department of defense. in the interest of the public, i'm asking for clarification on what's there, if anything, that's been developed because we don't need a new wuhan.
on behalf of the ppe club. mr president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, the transparency of the democratic system is crucial because it allows citizens to participate in decision-making. transparency is at the heart of all european policies, and among its most important elements is the right of citizens to request and obtain access to documents held by the european institutions. in principle, all documents of an institution should be made available to the public. on the other hand, certain documents should be treated in a special way because of their highly sensitive content. in this context, certain public and private interests should be specifically protected by way of derogation, as provided for in the regulation on public access to eu institutions' documents. the institutions shall have the right, where necessary, to protect their consultations and deliberations in order to ensure the performance of their tasks. the current legal framework of the regulation on public access to documents of the institutions is generally satisfactory, but we need to make further efforts to ensure that decisions are taken as openly as possible and as close to the citizen as possible. openness allows citizens to participate more actively in the decision-making process and ensures that the administration acquires greater legitimacy and is more efficient and accountable to eu citizens. in that sense, i think the commission should be commended in general for its transparency after all these figures we have heard today. of course, when it comes to transparency, there's been a lot of talk about joint vaccine procurement. i would point out that this has accelerated the process of developing and making available vaccines, and the union has achieved a better negotiating position and we have got more vaccines under better conditions and at a better price. also, joint procurement has avoided competition between member states, allowing smaller and less developed countries equal access to the vaccine, and i think that this whole process can indeed be characterized as a success. of course, in case of any uncertainties and doubts - everything that exists needs to be clarified, but without calling into question the whole procedure and i really look forward to the commission providing an answer to these dilemmas related to smes. finally, i would like to point out that the european union, led by the commission, has done the most important thing: it has successfully responded to this pandemic. equal access to vaccines for all european citizens is ensured, and this sends a strong message of unity and solidarity at european level.
mr president, transparency and public access to documents is at a low level in europe. the corona crisis, unfortunately, showed us that very clearly. it failed with regard to the purchase agreements for covid vaccines. why cannot we, as meps, have access to the purchase agreements? first, it was in a committee: we in the envi committee had a chance to see the agreements but were not allowed to scan, copy, take a photo or tell anybody about it. this is ridiculous and was a humiliation of members of the european parliament. then it was really published, but it was published in a redacted version, leaving everything important covered in black ink. at the end there were text messages between the ceo of pfizer and the president of the commission, and even the european ombudsman had to take action on this. one could get the impression, from the media, that they are more in a friendly relationship than in a professional one. instead of being a role model, the commission is clearly mocking the citizens.
dear chairman, commissioners, colleagues, the cathedral of st. james in šibenik, the church of st. krševan in zadar, the historic centre of dubrovnik, the banjski dvor in zagreb - all together, in 1861, an immovable cultural monument was destroyed due to the serbian aggression against the republic of croatia, the aim of which was to abolish the croatian national identity, built over centuries. that is why we in croatia know how important it is to persevere in protecting cultural heritage, especially in wartime circumstances. unfortunately, we are witnessing a continuing policy of erasure and denial of armenian cultural heritage in nagorno-karabakh and its surroundings, in violation of international law and the recent decision of the international court of justice. this is another case of the persecution of christians and suppression of their religious identity that is, unfortunately, the case in many parts of the world. globally, in 2019, more than 245 million christians were victims of severe persecution, 40 million more than in 2018. this means that one in nine christians globally is experiencing persecution, and this is very, very unfortunately not talked about. i therefore welcome the motion for a resolution by which the european parliament strongly opposes the denial of armenian cultural heritage in and around nagorno-karabakh. ladies and gentlemen, the destruction of the cultural heritage in nagorno-karabakh must stop as soon as possible.
madam president, the aggression against ukraine has marked the beginning of a new era for our entire continent, an era of great geopolitical change, but also an era of uncertainty. we had an example of this uncertainty, unfortunately, two weeks ago in zagreb, my hometown, when a soviet-made military drone, over 14 meters long, over six tons heavy, crashed in zagreb, in a populated area, in the immediate vicinity of a student residence, and fortunately there were no fatalities. but regardless, this dangerous, unusual incident does a pretty good job of showing how close we are to war, and unfortunately, closer than we ever think. and that's why it's time to wake up from some kind of limbo. it's time for unity. it is time for loyalty in these most delicate moments for the whole european union, for the whole nato, but also for the whole european civilization because this is one serious reminder that freedom and peace even on the european continent can no longer be taken for granted and that we will have to be able to fight for freedom and peace here in europe, but also in croatia.
in writing. the war in ukraine has brought the european union and the entire western alliance closer together than ever before. as a sign of this, us president biden will attend the eu summit tomorrow. the western alliance must do everything it can to put an end to putin's ruthless war crimes in ukraine. security policy cooperation must continue both within nato and beyond. the eu should continue to provide ukraine with more material and military assistance. the eu is now required to take a decision on the fifth round of sanctions against russia. you have to tighten the screws. the sanctions against both russia and belarus must be strengthened and extended to the energy sector, which supports the russian economy. and the rest of the banks have to be unplugged from swift. it is intolerable that we continue to finance putin's war by importing hundreds of millions of dollars of fossil fuels from russia every day. member states must stop importing russian gas, oil and coal. the commission's repowereu communication and presentations on gas security of supply and other contingency measures are welcome. they must be implemented quickly. the high energy prices must be responded to primarily by action at national level. no action shall be taken that would cause disruption to the emissions trading system or the electricity market. in the longer term, the key is to accelerate the transition to our own low-carbon energy solutions, including nuclear power. the russian aggression in ukraine has demonstrated the need for closer cooperation in the european union's defence and security policy. a strategic compass will give you a good guide. they must now be put into practice and strengthen the eu's role as the european pillar of nato.
