let me welcome the canadian prime minister, justin trudeau. thank you for joining us here today. le canada est un allié, un ami et un partenaire précieux. nous sommes des garants solides des valeurs de démocratie, d’état de droit et de paix – des valeurs que nous partageons. the enduring relationship between the european union and canada has withstood the test of time and only grows stronger. geography may divide us, but there is no ocean that can part our shared values, shared outlook and shared way of life. through our strategic partnership, and the comprehensive economic and trade agreement, our privileged relationship has reached new heights, but today we are facing a threat to all of us. we did not provoke putin’s illegal invasion of ukraine, we did not invite this confrontation between peace and war, but we share a commitment to solidarity with ukraine and the millions of people forced to flee putin’s bombs. there is so much at stake. no one knows this better than canada, that is home to one of the largest ukrainian communities in the world. the core values we all share are under attack. the democratic world order and the peace we have known are under threat. scenes we thought were confined to the dark chapters of our history books have returned. the fight of the ukrainian people is a fight for freedom, democracy and our way of life. we cannot leave them alone and we will not leave them alone. with millions forced to flee, the world is again staring at an ever-worsening humanitarian crisis that we must be ready – and we must be willing – to face. canada is a world leader in welcoming and resettling people in search of a life away from horror and we must rely on your expertise and your solidarity now again. it is really time to come together, now like never before. ‘whatever it takes’ means continuing to match massive sanctions with aid, military equipment and financial support. but it must also mean real humanitarian corridors, urgent relocation, air bridges and a redoubling of efforts to seek a ceasefire. that is the response that all our citizens – whether in canada or europe – expect. that is the response worthy of our place in history. this is our generation’s moment to prove we can live up to the promise of the world we inherited. we can give ukrainian children, women and men a chance at a future without fear, but we can only do this if the world acts together. dear prime minister trudeau, thank you for being here with us, for reaffirming your great country’s commitment to democracy, to equality, to multilateralism and to canada’s proud record of defending our shared humanity. mr prime minister, the floor is yours.
prime minister of canada. – madam president, i would like to thank you, david mcallister, chair of the committee on foreign affairs, and stéphanie yon—courtin, chair of the delegation for relations with canada. distinguished parliamentarians, ladies and gentlemen, it is a privilege to be here in brussels with you all. avant de commencer, j’aimerais d’abord exprimer mes condoléances à la suite du décès en début d’année du président sassoli. c’est une grande perte pour votre parlement et pour l’europe. j’aimerais également prendre un moment pour adresser mes condoléances aux proches des victimes de la tragédie de la louvière ainsi qu’à tous ceux dont la vie a été bouleversée par ces événements. nos pensées et notre amitié sont avec la belgique dans ces moments difficiles. i first addressed the european parliament in strasbourg in 2017. it was the first time a canadian prime minister addressed your parliament. back then, this parliament saw me as someone new on the world stage. we got to work tackling progressive issues, building the things that we thought mattered. we could see that something was lurking off on the horizon, something shadowy, something that was threatening. we didn’t look close enough. when i spoke to this parliament five years ago, i spoke about making multilateralism work for the middle class and those working hard to join it, so that economic frustrations don’t become fertile ground for populism, nativism, protectionism and the polarisation towards more extreme political views. well, today we are staring these forces in the face as they threaten the peace, justice, truth, democracy and stability of our world. we have a choice about what we do next. since 2017, the world has changed a lot. a global pandemic has taken the lives over of over six million people and disrupted the lives of everyone else. a global recession saw millions lose their jobs, with many economies still struggling to bounce back. it is a world that is no longer forecasting, but living, the dangers of climate change. there is a growing distrust of governments and facts, and a weakening of democratic institutions. we saw one of our staunchest guarantors of the rules—based order choosing for four years to step away from nato and away from multilateralism, and now we see putin’s criminal invasion of a sovereign, independent and democratic ukraine. people have a real and deep sense of uncertainty about what the future holds. these are anxious times, and people are looking for leadership and solutions. unfortunately, we’re seeing a rise in cynical populists who are trying to exploit these anxieties. they pretend to have easy solutions that play on people’s fears. even in canada, where 90% of people are vaccinated and our motto as a country is ‘peace, order and good government’, we saw anti—vaccine and anti-government protests devolve into illegal occupations of our communities and blockades of our borders. the leaders of those convoys were effective in turning citizens with real anxieties against the system best—suited to allay those concerns. but democracy isn’t a game, and there just aren’t easy solutions to the big, complex problems we are all facing. but that doesn’t mean that the way forward isn’t clear. there is no greater clarity than the will of the people who want better for themselves and their children at home, and no stronger defence against destructive forces from the outside, from outside our democracies, than the unwavering unity of like—minded partners. au cours des quatre-vingts dernières années, nous avons collectivement établi et renforcé une série de règles et d’institutions afin d’accroître la stabilité et la prévisibilité dans les affaires internationales. nous l’avons fait pour protéger les gens, pour leur offrir les meilleures perspectives de paix, pour défendre leur dignité et leurs droits inaliénables et pour faire en sorte qu’ils soient libres de choisir leur propre avenir. aujourd’hui, ces règles et institutions sont ouvertement menacées. vladimir poutine a enfreint les préceptes les plus fondamentaux du droit international, et il tue maintenant les civils innocents en bombardant des hôpitaux et des immeubles résidentiels. ce mépris flagrant des lois et de la vie humaine constitue une menace immense pour l’europe et pour le monde entier. canada, the eu and all of our partners and allies are facing a defining moment. we cannot fail. we must meet this moment. i speak to you today no longer as someone quite so new. in fact, i may now be one of the longer—serving progressive leaders around. i’m certainly not going to pretend to have all the answers, but what i do know is that there is work that lies before us as thoughtful leaders, as people focused on the short— and long—term well-being of all our citizens, and as people committed to democracy and the values that underpin them. that work is more important than ever. putin thought democracy was weak. he saw our disagreements and debates as weakness, but what he has never understood is that the rigours of debate, that forceful civic engagement, is what makes us strong, and that democracy at its best will always be stronger than authoritarianism. if we’re going to be honest, and we need to be honest in this place, we haven’t always been at our best these past years. putin thought we were divided. he thought he could weaken the eu and nato. he miscalculated, and he is seeing it backfire. nato and the eu are now more resolved and united than ever, because all of us in this room who are committed to democracy know in our bones that democracy didn’t happen by accident, and it will not continue without deliberate, mindful effort. mes amis, je veux être bien clair. les européens, y compris nos alliés en europe de l’est, peuvent compter sur l’amitié et le soutien total du canada. le canada dirige le groupement tactique de la présence avancée rehaussée de l’otan en lettonie, mission qui regroupe dix pays de l’otan. depuis cinq ans, des centaines de militaires canadiens sont déployés à des milliers de kilomètres de chez eux pour se positionner à 200 kilomètres de la frontière russe. ils sont déployés en lettonie, parce que ce qui est important pour la sécurité de l’europe l’est aussi pour la sécurité du canada. récemment, nous avons annoncé que nous doublions notre déploiement dans le cadre de l’otan pour les années à venir. ces militaires ne défendent pas seulement la lettonie et l’europe de l’est; ils défendent notre liberté, notre sécurité et toutes nos démocraties. we cannot let ukraine down. they are counting on us, so let’s use all the tools we have at our disposal. president zelenskyy and i have been talking often about the strong ties between our countries. canada, as mentioned, is home to the second largest ukrainian community in the world after russia, so we must all collectively step up to provide humanitarian aid, to support families affected by this war, and already start thinking about investing to rebuild ukraine afterwards. we must all continue to send military equipment and lethal aid to help ukrainians in their heroic defence, not just of their lands, but of all of the principles that defend ours. we must continue to impose unprecedented sanctions on putin and his enablers in russia and belarus, increasing the pressure as much as we can. we must ensure that the decision to invade a sovereign, independent country is understood to be a strategic failure that carries with it ruinous costs for putin and russia. putin’s attack on ukraine is an attack on the values that form the pillars of all democracies. we have a responsibility to make the case to people about why these values matter so much, not just to ukrainians, but to us all. we must recommit ourselves to the work of strengthening our democracies and demonstrate the principled leadership citizens everywhere are looking for. that leadership means reinforcing the things our citizens have in common, rather than playing to their differences. the task before us is not small, but given the right tools, we can do it. we should all be reassured of how much of this work is already underway in this place and in parliaments like it across europe and around the world. au canada, notre gouvernement élabore actuellement une nouvelle mesure législative pour lutter contre les méfaits en ligne. cette année, le canada assume la présidence de la coalition pour la liberté en ligne, et nous comptons mettre l’accent sur la protection des droits de la personne, l’inclusion et la diversité dans l’espace numérique. ici, à l’union européenne, votre devise est «unie dans la diversité». au canada, la diversité est aussi au cœur de notre identité. bien sûr, nous savons qu’il y a toujours plus de travail à faire, mais sur le plan de la réconciliation avec les peuples autochtones, de la lutte contre le racisme systémique, de l’égalité des sexes et de la défense soutenir les entrepreneurs des communautés noires, nous donnons des outils pour la croissance économique à ceux qui, trop souvent, ont été laissés pour compte. governments can and should play a positive role in people’s lives. in canada, like in many european countries, we made massive investments to respond to the covid—19 pandemic. it was the right thing to do to protect our people, and it was the necessary thing to do to ensure a strong and robust economic recovery. we know that when we invest in people, including in the most vulnerable, and when we grow the middle class and make life more affordable for everyone, not only do individuals benefit, but our entire country and the foundations of our democracy also benefit. que ce soit les conséquences immédiates de l’insécurité alimentaire, de l’inflation et des prix élevés de l’essence, ou que ce soit les conséquences à long terme du changement climatique ou de la pandémie, les démocraties doivent faire de leur mieux pour engager les conversations difficiles et nécessaires avec leurs citoyens afin de trouver des solutions. en matière de lutte contre le changement climatique, la collaboration entre le canada et l’union européenne continue d’être essentielle. notre travail se poursuit pour accélérer la transition vers une énergie verte et renouvelable, pour respecter nos engagements de la cop 26 et pour sécuriser nos chaînes d’approvisionnement, y compris pour les minéraux critiques. il faut continuer d’agir ensemble pour bâtir un avenir plus propre et une économie plus résiliente. canada and european countries also share the goals of reducing poverty and inequality while growing the middle class. we share aspirations for a better, safer and cleaner future. progressive trade agreements, like ceta, are helping our economies grow and creating good jobs while protecting high standards for workers, consumers and the environment. our ability to pull together, despite differences, and deliver for people really matters, and i thank you all for that. with the longest coastline in the world, canada’s shores reach out into the atlantic, pacific and arctic oceans. our connections with the whole of the americas is strong. we’re one of the most globally connected partners at the un, with memberships in nato, the g7, the g20, the arctic council, apec, the oas, the commonwealth and la francophonie. we in writing. the commission announced that it had prepared a fifth package of proposed sanctions against russia. so we have this paradox: the european union has also been a major player in the fight against terrorism, and it is not only the european union that is responsible for the attacks on the united nations, but also the european union itself. moreover, the russian state-owned company rosatom, which is controlled by the kremlin and is building turkey's first nuclear power plant in akkuyu, just a few kilometers from cyprus, is not included in the sanctions. the european citizens, particularly in cyprus, are well aware of the brutality and oppression of the erdoğan regime. the only ones behaving as if they don't know are eu officials, who have an obligation to know and act accordingly in the face of a brutal regime that continues to receive european funds. the european union's unanimous position on common defence and security and energy autonomy puts us in a position of responsibility in terms of strategic autonomy, since the threats are real. but common rules are needed, at the heart of which must be respect for international law, which has been violated for 42 days in ukraine by russian troops and for 48 years in cyprus, eu territory, by turkish occupation troops.
in writing. we adopted a resolution calling for a complete embargo on russian oil, coal, nuclear fuel and gas imports. the full and effective implementation of the sanctions already in place throughout the eu must be a priority for all member states. the european parliament also called on eu leaders to exclude russia from the g20 as well as from other multilateral cooperation organisations such as interpol, the world trade organisation and unesco. it would be a signal that the international community has no intention of restoring normal relations with the aggressor state. in order to strengthen the sanctions, parliament calls for the exclusion of russian banks from the swift system. the eu should also prohibit the entry of all vessels linked to russia and stop at eu ports and stop road transport from russia and belarus. meps call for the seizure of the assets of all russian officials and oligarchs linked to the putin regime and the revocation of their visas. he should also seize the assets of belarusian individuals associated with the lukashenka regime. parliament stressed that sanctions should be imposed on all russian officials, governors, mayors and business associates who support and benefit from the current policies of the putin regime. the resolution also calls on the eu to take action to prevent the spread of the virus and to ensure that the virus is contained in the country. the perpetrators of these crimes must be held accountable. parliament supported the inquiry launched by the prosecutor of the international criminal court into war crimes and crimes against humanity and the work of the commission of inquiry of the office of the high commissioner for human rights.
in writing. putin's brutal invasion of ukraine requires a strong and united eu position. the debate in plenary will be accompanied by the adoption of a joint resolution. although i strongly disagree with certain points in the joint resolution, particularly those concerning the continuation and strengthening of arms deliveries to ukraine and the general ban on the promotion of russian culture at events, i co-signed the joint resolution because i believe that there should be an unequivocal message of condemnation of the russian invasion. because at this moment, a strong, united message is needed for an immediate ceasefire and for a complete cessation of all russian aggressive military operations in ukraine, but this must be done without the use of military means. the joint resolution rightly supports the establishment of safe passageways and humanitarian corridors for the evacuation of civilians, the strengthening of eu humanitarian aid networks in ukraine, the investigation of alleged crimes against humanity and war crimes, the imposition of strict sanctions on russian oligarchs linked to the putin regime, compensatory measures and alternative supplies of goods and energy sources in order to reduce the negative impact on civilians, as well as effective measures against unacceptable profiteering, having, inter alia, drawn lessons from the pandemic response measures.
in writing. – russia’s invasion of ukraine and the brutal ways by which it has been put into effect have undermined almost completely all the historical and geopolitical claims that had been validly advanced to argue against nato expansion to the east. any military or diplomatic gains that russia might make from its so-called special military operation will not be legitimate. this apart from any accusations of war crimes that are being claimed against it. more immediately, european governments are adopting the right approach in focusing on sanctions, short of war, to deter the continuing military actions that russia, despite having suffered reverses on the ground, is still developing on a massive scale. in implementing sanctions, the eu must be careful not to impose punitive measures that end up wounding it more in the short to medium term than it does russia, for this might have counter-productive consequences in terms both of deterrence and of european public opinion. however it is also clear that the sharper and more biting that european sanctions become, the more effective their impact will be. balancing the different goals that need to be attained, is a delicate process that must achieve the optimal aim of bringing the ukraine war to a close a.s.a.p., hopefully today.
in writing. the russian military is committing monstrous atrocities in ukraine in the form of executions of civilians, rapes, attacks on civilian objects and forced displacement. we therefore need the member states of the european union to remain united and to make every effort, together with our democratic partners in the world, to stop the bloodshed in ukraine. the european union must send a clear signal to all countries in the world that helping russia circumvent economic sanctions or potentially supporting it militarily will have consequences for trade relations with europe. likewise, we must continue to impose all possible sanctions on russia, as well as on belarus. it is also necessary to allocate funds to help ukrainians who had to flee the war to the eu. last but not least, we must give the ukrainians hope for a european future and support them on their path to peace, freedom and democracy.
the invasion of ukraine by the russian federation is an attack on international law, the territorial integrity of a sovereign state and the self-determination of peoples. the atrocities committed against civilians in the context of the invasion are a crime against humanity which must be tried in the competent courts and lead to exemplary punishment. the european union has shown a strong unity not only in condemning the aggression but also in military and humanitarian support for the heroic resistance of ukraine, in welcoming refugees, in supporting the non-community countries which have actively collaborated in this welcome and in encouraging the citizens who in russia and belarus have taken up courageous positions condemning the war. successive sanctions packages that have had concrete impacts on punishing those responsible for the decision to massacre and destroy ukraine must continue in a more intense manner, focused on the most sensitive targets for those responsible and for the financing of the aggressor power's war effort, requiring the same level of commitment from all member states. at the same time, the european union must prepare, in a flexible and supportive manner, measures to respond effectively and fairly to the economic and social impacts of the war on its territory.
the rapporteur. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we are currently experiencing a turning point, triggered by this terrible, terrible war in ukraine. the coronavirus crisis and climate policy are not the focus of attention right now, and the same is true for digital policy. however, we are also witnessing a long overdue change in data and digital policy, which is beginning today with the adoption of the data governance act. to be honest, europe has missed the birth of the online platform economy and the associated revolution in the use of personal data. we left it to the big american companies like google, facebook, amazon, who are the first-timers who are still dominating the digital world today. but with the european data strategy, which was already announced in 2020, we in the european union have not only started to level the playing field in the digital world, but to fundamentally change the rules of the game. the data governance act, which we are now debating and voting on this afternoon, is the beginning of a paradigm shift that is necessary to build a growing, innovative european data economy as a counter-proposal to the approaches in the us or china. in the united states, data, ladies and gentlemen, is big business. the organization of the data space is left almost entirely to the private sector, which has also led to these considerable concentration effects. in china, data is almost state property. china has a combination of government surveillance and big tech companies that largely control huge amounts of data on their own. and we in europe, we're going a different way. we're all about the user. we have seen this for many years when we have been discussing the general data protection regulation to protect the rights of users in particular here. so this is a huge opportunity for europe to emancipated itself, i would say, in the digital economy, to go our own european way. we need it because the strength of our data economy in europe is also driven by small and medium-sized enterprises, by start-ups, to whom we must give a chance. the goal that the data governance act is pursuing is to break up data silos, to share data, to make data sharing easier, to make data more usable for our economy and our society. that is why we are creating the framework with the data governance act so that neutral data brokers can offer their services in the european union. data brokers who do not use the data entrusted to them for their own economic purposes, as the other large platform operators do, but these data brokers are only responsible for aggregating this data entrusted to them, refining it, preparing it in such a way that others can work well with this data provided. we believe that by doing so, we create a trusted environment that allows for fair data sharing, and we very much hope that this will be embraced in practice. i would like to conclude by expressing my sincere thanks to the colleagues who have been consulting this dossier intensively for many months - i see them all here before me. i thank you for the good cooperation and i believe that what was particularly important to us as the european parliament on this dossier, we were able to achieve only in the tough negotiations with the member states and with the help of the commission, because we were so united. we have now defined a clear scope of application which creates legal certainty. we have strengthened a european data innovation council. we have decided that there should be a common eu logo for data brokering services, a european single access point from which member states' data sets can also be searched across borders, and much more. i am proud, ladies and gentlemen, that we have been able to discuss this matter together so quickly. i think - and this is my last sentence - that the information revolution will not wait for europe and will certainly not come about through wishful thinking. we must act now! we must now do everything we can to design the data space in such a way that our citizens, our companies and our private sector can get the most out of it, with the legislative proposals still pending.
member of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i am very pleased to be able to take part today in the debate on the data governance act, following the agreement in principle between the co-legislators and the commission that we reached last november. i would first like to thank in particular the rapporteur, angelika niebler, dear angelika, for the absolutely outstanding work that has been done, mr lagodinsky of course, and all the fictitious rapporteurs for the excellent work that has been done on this matter, and also for the efficiency and speed with which the negotiations have been concluded. the political agreement was reached in just over a year after the publication of the commission's proposal and after only two political trilogues. this is a result which i would like to underline in your presence. this initiative is the first stone in the legislative framework announced as part of the data strategy, which aims to ensure that our internal market can anticipate but also accompany the data revolution that lies ahead. it is about building a genuine internal data market, open but also sovereign, which facilitates data sharing in line with our values. europe has the capacity to be a major player in the wave of innovation that will accompany the development of the industrial data economy. we know that the potential is enormous for employment, for european businesses and especially for small and medium-sized enterprises. by 2024, that's nearly 175 zettabytes of data that will be produced on our planet, or 175 trillion trillion pieces of data. the data economy, especially industrial data, will therefore be at the heart of the green and digital transitions we are experiencing. the aim is to organise the conditions for access and transfer of data so that they can circulate within the internal market, but also generate value, while guaranteeing a framework of trust for their use. this is the whole point of the data governance act. we will thus be able to help develop common european data spaces by ensuring that more data is available and also by strengthening voluntary data sharing mechanisms. the data governance act will therefore strengthen the trust needed for data sharing and also reduce the cost of transactions: on the one hand, by regulating neutral data intermediaries whose mission is to connect those who have data with those who want to use it; on the other hand, by regulating neutral data intermediaries whose mission is to connect those who have data with those who want to use it; on the other hand, by regulating neutral data intermediaries whose mission is to provide information to those who need it; on the other hand, by regulating neutral data intermediaries whose mission is to provide information to those who need it; on the other hand, by regulating neutral data intermediaries whose mission is to provide information to those who need it; on the other hand, by regulating neutral data intermediaries whose mission is to provide information to those who need it. the establishment of a european data innovation committee, in the form of an expert group, which will promote the emergence of standards for cross-sectoral interoperability; finally, and i will conclude with this, by protecting sensitive european data against any unlawful access, including by a government or a third country. i would therefore like to thank parliament again for its support for this first act. i would also like to point out that, at the same time, we have also made very good progress on the framework for the use of data by the so-called 'gatekeepers' (access controllers) in the dma, which was recently concluded, as you know, in a trilogue. i believe that these two texts therefore constitute an excellent basis for cooperation in the discussions we are about to have on the data act, which the commission presented a few weeks ago.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. madam president, an innovative and sovereign europe is making its entrance onto the world stage. as such, the data governance act appears to be the most sustainable and relevant solution to exploit the enormous potential of data. we need to promote and secure data sharing without forcing anyone to do so. this is how we will multiply transnational opportunities, for our citizens, for our businesses and for the altruistic organisations that do the common good. with the dga, we will offer a european model and an alternative to the data processing practices of the big platforms in the us or the chinese government. yes, ladies and gentlemen, we are going to take back control and create a safer space, which will finally allow european actors to access a large amount of data. this final agreement is the result of a great teamwork, and i would like to thank our colleague angelika niebler and commissioner thierry breton. it is now up to us to build a market for non-personal data and to establish benchmarks that will, i am sure, radiate all over the world.
madam president, first of all i would like to thank the commissioner for his presence and his proposal and, above all, for the excellent work of the rapporteur, mrs niebler, with whom i of course share the conviction that our union absolutely needs a data governance act: a law that increases trust in data intermediaries and strengthens the mechanisms for sharing; a law that focuses on the protection, privacy and confidentiality of data, and also on the reuse of some of it held by the public sector. it is also essential to ensure that micro, small and medium-sized enterprises and start-ups do not face any artificial restrictions or obstacles: public data should be available to them at all times and, where possible, free of charge or at reduced rates. we therefore naturally support the interim agreement, since it also includes, among other things, the possibility for public sector bodies to conclude new contracts allowing for the exclusive right to reuse data held by them for 12 months instead of the three years originally envisaged in the commission's proposal, or also the establishment of special conditions applicable to the transfer of non-personal data held by the public sector which are considered to be highly sensitive and where the transfer could jeopardise the objectives of the union or involve the risk of re-identification of such data.

on behalf of the renew group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, every second billions of data are generated by machines of all kinds. they represent a very important asset which, if put to good use, can make life easier for citizens, improve services or aid research. a heritage which we must above all preserve from misuse or illegal use. data governance is therefore one of the great challenges facing policy in the digital age. we have worked in recent months, first as parliament - and i thank mrs niebler, the rapporteur for this measure - together with the council, then - and thanks to the important mediation of the commission and commissioner breton - to provide europe with a legal instrument which will enable us to regulate the sharing of data, to get ahead of our partners and to lead the game in this field. data that underpins the development of artificial intelligence and the economy of tomorrow. the aim is to create a kind of 'data schengen', as some call it, for the benefit of individuals and businesses, and especially small and medium-sized enterprises, which will have greater control over the sharing and management of data. we are talking about a tool which will enable operators of all sizes, especially the smaller ones, to access a market for intermediary services based on transparency and neutrality, a tool which aims to regulate the reuse of data held by the public sector, which are too often unused. finally, this regulation creates an innovation committee with several tasks, the first of which is to help increase data interoperability in the european union and to establish guidelines for the creation of common european data spaces. in short, thanks to this important tool, we aim to have more and more data available, with more and more safeguards, being careful to balance, on the one hand, rules and principles and, on the other, not to hinder an economy that is all emerging and evolving.
on behalf of the left group. madam president, i would also like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for the inclusive way in which she has conducted this whole process. this legislation that we are voting on here today is important and necessary. provided that data protection is guaranteed in accordance with european law, data sharing has enormous potential in the area of research and innovation and can generate important social and economic benefits. with this regulation we can increase data sharing in the union, especially for the benefit of civil society, cooperatives and small and medium-sized enterprises. we do, however, have some reservations about the final text. the sharing of data on workers, which had been eliminated in parliament's version, is now removed from the agreement. the concept of general interest in the field of data altruism or data sharing with third countries has become even more undefined. the monitoring of all processes, which was ensured by public agencies and institutions, is now handed over to private parties. we are moving increasingly towards a model of governance which, in our view, does not protect the fundamental interests of the citizens of the european union. however, this is an important proposal, as i said, and therefore, because the final agreement fell short of parliament's position, we shall abstain.

madam president, commissioner, madam rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, this new regulation on european data governance is of obvious importance because it determines the legal framework for the reuse of personal and non-personal data, either by non-profit organisations, so-called data altruists, voluntarily provided for general interest purposes such as medical research, or by commercial companies, data sharing service providers, and only if they do not use it for their own purposes. it is clear that the european union, which has fallen considerably behind the usa and china in this area, must have the means to impose its own systems for the management, use and protection of data of individuals, administrations and companies in each state. we would therefore tend to support this first step towards harmonizing the rules. but the system is fragile, because if reusable data is supposed to be anonymised, the fact remains that the european union is once again choosing the path of openness and competition, preventing any national sovereignty over vital data for each state. as for volunteering, it will only exist for the duration of this first project, because the question of relations with all public or private structures that will not participate in the sharing of data will soon arise, some having been sparked by the example of the gaia-x fiasco, an exciting european project, which was open to companies that had worked with the cia or the nsa. so while we obviously support the general approach, and despite the data protection regulation, we are sorry, we will abstain on this matter.

madam chair, the right to leave is often abused. illegal migration is a major problem for the european asylum system and in this regard i would point out that the republic of croatia, with more than 6 500 police officers, is effectively protecting the longest external land border of the european union. last year, over 17 000 cases of persons found to have crossed the border illegally were recorded in croatia, which is 40% less than in 2020. this is a testament to the ability of the croatian police and the readiness of croatia, which is why the slovenian police are recording a decrease in the number of illegal migrants. the commission has also taken note of the fact that the council has not yet adopted a decision on the accession of croatia to the schengen area. i stress that croatia has an obligation to protect the state border, which it does in accordance with european and international law. in this regard, i would remind you that an independent mechanism has been established to monitor the conduct of police officers. the prevention of illegal migration is crucial for the protection of the european way of life and the croatian police, in accordance with the rules, exercise police powers. any isolated incidents are the subject of an internal procedure and in no case constitute systematic action by police officers. in conclusion, i call on all member states to step up their efforts to tackle illegal migration because the border is crossed at border crossings, not on forests and rivers.
madam speaker, four million ukrainians are fleeing russian aggression, fleeing russian shells because their homes have been destroyed, because they have no food, no water, and because they are fleeing together with their families. ninety percent of them are women and children. europe is clearly showing its heart, showing its solidarity, just as we have shown before. we hear in this debate today that it shows some kind of hypocrisy, that it shows some kind of double standards or even that it shows some kind of racist policy that europe supposedly has. that couldn't be further from the truth. this is a situation where a neighboring country is at war, where there is a completely different legal basis and where we have an obligation to protect the border from illegal migration in other cases. we are humane to all those who seek our protection, especially those fleeing conflict, but we must remain resolute, we must remain firm against those who abuse the system, and we must be especially uncompromising to those who profit from such a system.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, freedom of movement in europe is one of the key achievements of the european union. the pandemic has shown once again how vulnerable that freedom can be, and also that we cannot take that freedom for granted. on the contrary, to strengthen it, it is essential that all countries adhere to the schengen acquis and continue to build the trust on which schengen is based. the application of the schengen acquis is a legal obligation for all the member states of the european union, and it is clear that today the world, the circumstances are completely different from when schengen was founded. the world is more complex, security is more at risk, and dramatic global changes are changing the way schengen is applied. despite all the global challenges, croatia has met all the conditions through over 280 technical recommendations for the application of the schengen acquis, which was confirmed by the commission, but also unanimously by the council in december last year. over the past seven years, we have shown and proved that we can protect europe's external borders in accordance with the highest standards of protection, but also with respect for human rights, and implement all the measures necessary for the functioning of schengen. it's time for croatian citizens to finally enjoy full freedom of movement in europe because croatia is ready for schengen!
mr president, a strong and united europe, a europe without borders, a europe which, despite this unfortunate war in ukraine, implements its strategic decisions and realizes its objectives only such a europe can survive and only such a europe can prosper in the future and only in such a europe without borders can citizens feel the meaning of the european union, and croatia is technically ready for schengen. that is what the commission and the council say. ladies and gentlemen, when you talk to people in croatia today, for them schengen is not just about connectivity and faster border crossing. today, in the context of this war in ukraine, it becomes a question of whether we are all the same european family or not. for the citizens of croatia, it becomes a security and existential issue. delaying such a decision to bring croatia into schengen would only benefit those who hope that europe is shaken, wounded, weak and divided. and that's why we're going to prove you wrong!
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, more than 3.9 million people have fled ukraine since the beginning of the russian aggression, taking refuge in the various territories of the european union. this is a figure which is unfortunately set to increase in the coming weeks, given the prolonged conflict. this is an emergency which affects women and children in particular and which has been very well received, particularly by certain states which are often instrumentally demonized in this house, and i am referring to poland, romania, slovakia, hungary and moldova. the commission has also proposed that the member states should be able to provide the necessary financial support to the countries concerned. that is why our group, although aware that these resources may not be sufficient and may require further intervention in the coming weeks, as it has already done with the care package, strongly supports and has strongly supported this measure and this procedure, considering them essential in relation to the increase in the prefinancing of react-eu to support the member states, but above all to continue to demonstrate our closeness to the ukrainian people. once again, cohesion policy, as it was already the case with the pandemic, is proving to be the most effective and flexible policy with which to deal with emergencies and provide support to the different member states. and it is precisely because of this capacity that i think we need to think more broadly, because it is clear that we need to build a structural solution to prevent these resources from being used in an excessive way. the commission has an urgent structural solution to find in order to guarantee a solution and a constant response to a problem which will not be solved in a few days or a few months. that is why we will continue to support this type of intervention in relation to the work and the emergencies we will face.

the debate is closed. the vote will take place today, thursday 7 april 2022. written declarations (rule 171)
in writing. as a result of russia's unprovoked and unjustified military aggression, the european union and in particular several of its eastern regions are facing a number of challenges. the biggest challenge remains the millions of people fleeing the war. the remarkable solidarity of the european peoples in welcoming refugees has resonated around the world. russia's aggression against ukraine is putting additional financial pressure on member states, especially on frontline states. adequate funding is needed to support refugees immediately. in this context, cohesion policy will advance eur 3,4 billion from react-eu to member states, especially for frontline member states, so that they can provide support to all refugees. for all member states, the commission proposes to increase the pre-financing from 11% to 15% of the react-eu tranche for 2021. the commission proposes raising the rate to 45% for frontline member states. the proposal allows for a reduction in the pressure on member states' public budgets so that they can manage the flow of refugees from ukraine. the european union and each member state, in particular romania and poland, are close to the suffering of the ukrainian people.
in writing. russian military aggression against ukraine has had terrible impacts on the ukrainian territory and its people, who are suffering immensely. the consequences of this unjust conflict are felt far beyond the borders of ukraine, affecting the world, particularly europe, in multiple dimensions. the humanitarian dimension is undoubtedly the most important and the eu has shown enormous solidarity since the start of this conflict, welcoming swiftly millions of refugees from ukraine. but to respond adequately to reception needs, member states need more than current programmes to fund migrant integration projects. they also need mechanisms similar to those that have enabled faster responses to the pandemic. in this regard, and as a way of supporting member states in the additional challenges posed to their public budgets, the increase in the pre-financing of the reacteu resources is of great importance, but should be seen in complementarity with other action measures. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the eu's new rules on the protection of the environment and the fight against climate change, and will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules on the protection of the environment and the fight against climate change.
the next item on the agenda is the commission statement on the situation of marginalised roma communities in the eu (2022/2573 ((rsp)).
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, the eu strategic framework for equality, inclusion and participation sets out a comprehensive three-pillar approach for the next 10 years. the scope of the strategy was reinforced by the council recommendation adopted in march 2021. the framework’s seven objectives and headline targets allow for effective monitoring and measuring of the progress achieved by member states. as requested, most member states have adopted new national roma strategic frameworks. the commission is currently assessing them and will publish a report on the implementation at national level later this year. more work is needed, of course, to improve the social and economic inclusion of marginalised roma communities. the roma survey conducted by the eu agency for fundamental rights, found that 80% of roma continue to live below the ‘at risk of poverty’ threshold of their country. every third roma lives in housing without tap water. one in 10 in housing, without electricity and every fourth roma, that is 27%, and every third roma child, that is 30%, lives in a household that faced hunger at least once in the previous month. when asked if the total household income is sufficient to make ends meet, 92% of roma surveyed indicate that they face at least some difficulties in this regard, with 4 to 5% facing great difficulties. the european social fund plus (esf+), part of eu cohesion policy for years 2021-2027, has, under its policy objective four, a more social and inclusive europe, a specific objective for funding the socio-economic integration of marginalised communities such as roma people. i call on member states to use the good opportunity provided by the current momentum, when the partnership agreements and cohesion policy programmes are about to be adopted, to ensure that the marginalisation, exclusion and segregation of roma is addressed in the most effective ways. this is particularly relevant for member states with a significant roma population. through its citizens equality, rights and values programme, the european commission makes available eur 12 million for projects combating all forms of discrimination. the objective of the specific calls for proposals that are issued is to promote equality and to fight against racism, xenophobia and discrimination, including combating manifestations of anti-gypsyism. with the outbreak of russia’s military aggression towards ukraine, millions of people are fleeing the war and seeking refuge, and amongst them are also many roma people. more than 4 million persons already sought shelter in eu countries bordering ukraine, of whom 100 000 are estimated to be roma. the european union is helping these people through direct humanitarian aid, emergency civil protection assistance, support at the border by eu agencies, as well as an immediate protection status for those fleeing the war. we must ensure that our support continues to reach all those who need it. we are also ensuring that member states have the necessary liquidity to address the most urgent needs such as schooling, housing and hospital beds. the commission had set up a solidarity platform to coordinate operational and financial support and reception capacity. nevertheless, we must be aware that roma fleeing the war often struggle with additional vulnerabilities, such as a lack of identification documents, separation from family members, language-related communication difficulties and biased attitudes at the borders. according to civil society reports, roma were accused of not being genuine refugees, of taking advantage of the availability of humanitarian aid, or that they intend to abuse the welfare system of eu countries. we must ensure that people can always flee wars even if they do not have an identification document, and that the most vulnerable ones are provided humanitarian help in an unhindered manner. during my mandate, i prioritised dialogue with civil society. and this is done via regular consultation fora with stakeholders at european level and also within the context of the european platform for roma inclusion, which ensures cooperation at the highest political level. i remain committed to visiting roma in their communities and in their homes to learn directly from them and take that knowledge with me to meetings with national and regional politicians.

mr president, the government's policy of isolation rather than integration is preventing the roma from being integrated into normal life. it keeps them isolated in their communities, where they live lives that are unacceptable to most citizens. roma settlements are often filled with violence, garbage, abandoned animals. they are mostly uneducated and prone to excess. young girls are forced to marry very early. at 15, they have more children because the government gives them benefits, but they don't have the opportunity to go to school. it's no secret that in roma settlements, say in međimurje, weapons are hidden - from pistols to automatic rifles and explosive devices, and there has been police intervention. children sometimes come to school with knives - there have been cases like this, and instead of giving roma the opportunity to work and contribute to society through employment programs, they are given money and white technology. this technique is mostly sold, the money is spent on addictive behavior, often in gambling, which gives kids from a young age bad parenting, bad role models, and they don't know any better. young roma who have broken out of this vicious circle and found employment are building homes and careers outside roma settlements. although most young people in croatia will tell you that they want a different life and that they want to move out of the roma settlements, the policy of the croatian governments is still to keep them in their settlements, without much chance and conditions for a better life.
dear chairman, commissioner, colleagues and colleagues. research has shown that 77 percent of consumers would rather repair than replace a defective product. however, there are many obstacles that prevent consumers from repairing the product. the commission also found that the union industry had not been able to obtain sufficient spare parts to meet the demand for the product concerned. and i also get complaints from consumers that the household products that their parents bought lasted longer than the products that we buy today. in order to enable citizens to have the right to repair a product, it is therefore important to ensure the availability of spare parts and to enable consumers to obtain information on the expected life of the product under normal use and the possibility of its repair when purchasing it. in addition, i call on the commission to consider the possibility of extending the guarantee when proposing a legislative act, and certainly to blacklist practices that unjustifiably restrict the right to repair or lead to product defects as being absolutely unfair commercial practices. it is the minimum we must do to protect the rights of consumers in europe. thank you very much.
on behalf of the ppe club. dear chair, commissioners, colleagues, mental disorders are one of the greatest public health challenges. their widespread prevalence, the burden they place on health systems, indicate the need for action at multiple levels. in 2018, approximately 13,5% of all hospital beds in the eu were psychiatric care beds, and this practice has certainly increased during the pandemic. scientists point out that mental problems are often a more significant cause of illness and premature death than are physical illnesses. this data points to the need to invest in mental health, including early detection and treatment of disorders, thereby contributing to the well-being and economic benefits of individuals and society. the covid-19 pandemic and the economic crisis that followed have put a heavy burden on the mental health of citizens, especially young people and those from lower income groups. our citizens felt insecurity, a change in daily routine, fear of existence and social isolation. according to the world health organization, in the first year of the pandemic, the number of cases of anxiety and depression rose by a whopping 25 percent, which is extremely worrying. the commission should also be able to provide information on the implementation of the union's policy on asylum and immigration, including on the implementation of the union's policy on asylum and immigration. what i would particularly like to stress is that we need a common strategy for mental health, following the european plan to fight cancer. it is essential to increase the effectiveness and accessibility of mental health systems at european level. we need to encourage interdisciplinary collaboration, as well as increasing the integration of mental health care into primary health care. this will contribute to the development of health services that provide more targeted and coordinated care for people with mental disorders. the establishment of a knowledge-sharing network is also needed for the effective implementation of best practices. in conclusion, i would stress the need for greater investment by the member states, but also by the european union, in mental health, i.e. programmes to protect and promote mental health, especially in children and young people. thank you very much.

dear chair, dear commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, artificial intelligence is already around us today, even more than we are aware of, in many everyday applications, applications and services, from social networks and internet search engines to medical diagnostics or smart transport. in any case, ai is already today the central technology of the new digital era, with the potential to fundamentally change and transform our societies. it is also clear that its widespread application does not come without danger. we have heard and heard a lot about protecting the fundamental rights of citizens in this debate today, and it is quite clear that we must expect new technologies to respect human dignity above all and to respect all those democratic european values in which we believe. at the same time, our development depends on our ability to adapt to new, often cruel times, the cruel times in which we live. we therefore need an approach that encourages uptake, that encourages european competitiveness and a faster and better uptake of new technologies. we owe it to the new generations to offer them exactly that, so that a high-quality, good life in europe is possible. this is not possible without the application of new technologies, it is not possible without the application of artificial intelligence. at the same time, we owe them a responsibility to manage our society and manage our future. so in any case, we support the right, efficient, better, higher quality application of ai, but with all those safeguards that will ensure that the human being remains at the center of its application and that we have a normal, european, balanced approach between those extremes that we unfortunately see when we look at the global situation.
blue-card speech. – so you said that people want us to resolve issues and problems. i agree with that. you said that people want to have bigger incomes, minimum pensions and things like that. i agree also with that. but why do think that unanimity will achieve that? why do think that getting rid of unanimity will lead the member states to actually share more powers with the smaller member states and that they will be willing to resolve problems. because i personally think if we have more qualified majority, the member states will have more powers to decide what is in their interests and not to share the powers and responsibilities with smaller member states. so why would people in, for instance, croatia have better results if we get rid of the possibility for croatia to have veto rights in the council?
the conference on the future of europe confirmed that citizens want greater powers for the european union in the field of health, the creation of a european data space to enable the exchange of health data and facilitate cross-border healthcare, the achievement of the european union's strategic autonomy in the production of medicines and a better system for deciding on the conditions of major health threats are priorities of european citizens. these are problems that member states cannot really solve alone and joint action is necessary in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity. i am therefore pleased that by creating the european health union we are already responding positively to these demands. on the other hand, the conference suggested that unanimity in decision-making should be replaced almost entirely by qualified majority decision-making. there should be clarity. the right of veto is a useful instrument that ensures that member states protect their interests in certain strategic areas such as tax, social, military and so on. this is particularly important for smaller member states which, even in a common europe, have the right to preserve their own rights and identity. that's why we refrain from experiments that can only and exclusively create harm.

the main challenges faced by the islanders, mr president, commissioners, colleagues and friends, are the lack of basic infrastructure, poor transport links with the mainland, high prices, a shortage of labour and a lack of sustainable economic activities that are not exclusively seasonal. all of this makes the island areas vulnerable to depopulation and underdevelopment, which is a huge problem. we must fight this with cohesion policy, with measures for economic development and with improvements in the availability of public services on the islands, and this very good report speaks to that. we need to offer the people of the islands a different perspective, beyond the tourist one, so that they are not completely dependent on the success or failure of the summer season. the commission has also proposed that the european union should provide a financial contribution to the development of the island of madeira, in particular through the european regional development fund. secondly, we need to encourage health workers to come and work on the islands and, in general, create a framework that will allow people to stay on the islands, especially our young people. finally, i would stress the importance of developing transport links and broadband internet as public services to help overcome the isolation faced by islanders. cohesion policy must be flexible enough to adapt to the specific problems of islands, because they are an important part of the european identity and we need to make them attractive for life in the 21st century.

mr. vice president, vice president, ladies and gentlemen, the founding treaties have always attracted public attention and been the subject of controversy. in this sense, the conference on the future of europe has gone a step further by enabling a number of citizens to express their own vision of the future of the union. in the context of a pandemic that has affected all member states, it is therefore not surprising that citizens want to increase the powers of the european union in relation to health by amending the treaty. as an mep who is active in health policy-making, i strongly support strengthening the eu's role in health by creating a european health union. in particular, i believe that the protection of public health, particularly in relation to those problems which cannot be solved by the states alone, should be included in article 4, but also that article 168 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union should be amended, thereby strengthening the eu's competences in this area. on the other hand, caution should be exercised in reforming the decision-making system, especially if such reform would involve replacing the unanimity system with qualified majority decision-making. the right of veto ensures that member states' interests are protected in their strategic areas, and this is particularly important for smaller member states, which also have the right to preserve their own rights and identity in a common europe. i am therefore not inclined to experiment and believe that the european union can achieve its key objectives within the framework of the existing decision-making system. the vast majority of union acts are already adopted by the ordinary legislative procedure by a qualified majority of the member states, and that is quite sufficient.

vice-president of the commission. – mr president, only a few comments. first of all, the commission aims at adopting the legislative proposal after the summer break, as many of you asked. the initiative requires urgent action, which does not allow us to prepare an impact assessment, but the commission will publish the evidence, as i said earlier, in a staff working document. we published a call for evidence on 23 may, and it is open for public feedback until 24 june. we encourage stakeholders to submit comments. all of us know that combating forced labour is nothing new. our trade agreements commit our partners to signing and effectively implementing the ilo fundamental conventions, including on forced labour. company law instruments and proposals oblige companies to report on forced labour and address it in their operations and supply chains. moreover, our anti—trafficking directive from 2011 ensures that member states penalise human trafficking and forced labour. and to conclude, i do appreciate the valuable comments and questions raised in this debate today. it is clear that we have the same objective. combating forced labour and promoting due diligence standards are priorities of the european union’s agenda on business and human rights. we are looking forward to further exchanges with you on this issue. thank you mr president, thank you honourable members, thank you mr lange, so we are looking forward to having legislative proposals soon.

year with severe drought, record temperatures, devastating floods, tropical storms in temperate areas, landslides and all the other extreme phenomena that have taken between 90 and 140 thousand lives in europe in recent decades. hundreds of journalists, activists, integrity watchers have been attacked and even killed while trying to protect forests and natura 2000 areas from loggers. ignoring the issue of illegal logging, we vote against greenhouse emissions. we do not want to change anything that is happening now in the union, not just in romania, hungary or austria. we're not defending these people, their families, their children. we don't vote with the citizens in mind, who we have a duty to represent, but with individual and group negotiations in mind, with party lines, beliefs or personal interests in mind. and that is why citizens all over the union are punishing us, punishing us with absenteeism, with vote-blaming, with support for extremist parties and anti-european currents. now, we all know that illegal logging is not just environmental crime. the degree of destruction and the levels at which this scourge affects us is a crime, and the victims are not just us, but all future generations. european citizens have understood this long ago. i'm sending it to us through a number of petitions and initiatives that i'm sure you're all receiving. if you think that illegal logging doesn't exist in your country, you're wrong. what is cut in other european countries ends up in the single market, what is stolen in one member state costs us all - billions and billions of euros a year. if you think that this scourge is punishable in your country, europol shows you with figures that you are wrong. if you think that the polluted environment, the destroyed biodiversity and all the climate change stops at your borders, again, you're wrong. and why then do some colleagues and some parliamentary groups still refuse to punish illegal logging at european level? that's what i'm wondering. the citizens are also asking. that's what the press asks us after every vote in the committees and in the plenary. why is it that in 2022 we still do not have a single european regime and a specialised european institution for such a crime? why is it that in an area where the union has competence we do not want to legislate, even though this is our mandate given by vote from the european citizens who pay us? i have called for and insisted on the establishment of the european green prosecutor because illegal logging in one country affects the whole union. please support this motion for a resolution and the associated question as additional steps to move closer to the objectives of the green plan and to combat the environmental crimes that kill european citizens. it's affecting all of our health and it's jeopardizing our children's future.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, first i would like to thank the petitions committee for adding this oral question to this week’s plenary agenda and for giving us the opportunity to discuss today this highly important topic. let me start by stressing the commission is strongly committed to protecting and restoring existing forests worldwide and that the protection of forests features very highly on our political agenda. this eu forest strategy, which we adopted last year, which is anchored in both the european green deal and the eu diversity strategy, builds on the multiple roles played by forests for the climate, biodiversity, society and the economy. it strives to ensure that our forests deliver on all those fronts. our goal is to overcome the challenges related to climate change and biodiversity loss and at the same time to promote the bio—economy to unlock the potential of forests for our future. if we want the european union to succeed in its transition towards sustainability, we will need forests that are bigger, healthier and more resilient than today. in this spirit, the forest strategy shows how increased climate and biodiversity ambitions can go hand-in-hand with economic prosperity. it is a realistic strategy, which also acknowledges that our forests are under threat. we have seen how, during the pandemic, illegal logging actually increased in the european union. the eu forest strategy for 2030 states explicitly that the implementation and enforcement of our relevant acquis to protect forests needs to be stepped up, and the commission is determined to play its role as guardian of the treaties. over the past years, where we concluded that eu legislation was not properly implemented, we have launched infringement procedures against the member states concerned, for example, poland, slovakia, romania and estonia. in your oral question, you ask how the commission plans to assess the extent of illegal logging and the economic losses it causes within the european union. in relation to the economic losses linked to illegal logging, we do not have official recent estimates, but we can see from various data sources that it could be substantial. a study of the world bank gives estimates of foregone tax revenue due to illegal logging for 2017 europe without russia. it estimates that between usd 19 million and usd 380 million in tax income was lost. the eu timber regulation remains the eu’s primary tool for preventing illegally logged timber entering eu markets. the commission assesses the correct enforcement of eu timber regulation by member states on the basis of their annual reporting of relevant, substantiated concerns and trade data. in addition, the commission relies on satellite images available to evaluate illegal logging in protected natura 2000 sites, and on reports from civil society on the ground. we will continue to work closely with member states to develop other relevant initiatives and tools, including under the framework of the regulation of deforestation, which the commission proposed in november 2021. the regulation of deforestation will eventually replace the timber regulation and expand reporting obligations by member states. it proposes reporting on the content of checks, their follow up, country of origin and production of timber. this will allow the commission to better assess the quality of member states’ enforcement activities. let me now turn to the second part of your oral question concerning the compliance of member states with their commitments to restore areas deteriorated by illegal logging. the commission has opened several infringements against member states to address illegal logging that has taken place in natura 2000 sites in breach of the habitats directive. in these cases, the commission has asked member states to restore deteriorated areas and to present a restoration plan. on this basis, the commission will carefully assess the delivery of such commitments using both satellite intelligence and field evidence as appropriate. lastly, on the publishing of the detailed assessment of all forest management plans in member states, we plan to do this through the new legislative instrument on eu forest monitoring plans for 2023. furthermore, the assessment will consider how to support and advice foresters in setting up those plans. in parallel, the commission is using geospatial intelligence to assess individual cases where there might be a risk to compliance. for example, geospatial intelligence is increasingly being used by the commission to assess complaints about natural forest habitat deterioration in special areas of conservation. finally, the commission is also developing the eu observatory on deforestation and forest degradation, which investigates changes in the world’s forest cover and associated drivers. honourable members, thank you for your attention, and i look forward to your questions and comments.

and that is the main purpose of the budget amendment and the report i am presenting to you today. the changes provide the bulk of the 400 million euros to help receive and register ukrainian refugees. concrete solidarity, financial, logistical, humanitarian aid, as well as any other form of support to the ukrainian people who are fighting for their freedom, for their survival, is a human and moral obligation of the whole of europe. the brutal russian aggression that can have no justification will leave long-lasting consequences not only on ukraine, but also on europe and on our european finances. we are aware of this and therefore must take all measures in time to mitigate the consequences, whether it is to care for refugees, to ensure energy supplies or to protect food supplies. the after nine years of membership of the european union, croatia is on the verge of achieving another strategic objective. the accession to the euro area will remove transaction costs and currency risk, from which croatian exporters and tourism will benefit the most. the very announcement of the euro has already led to an increase in the national credit rating, which allows for lower interest rates for businesses and citizens. also, by this step, as well as by joining schengen, croatia has shown a will for even closer integration into the european community of peoples and states. in general, we can say that joining the eurozone represents a great benefit for the croatian economy and society as a whole. meeting the conditions for the introduction of the euro, especially those related to public debt and deficits, was not easy. the commission notes that the hungarian authorities have not provided any evidence that the debt was not repaid. we have shown that economic policy can be conducted in a way that promotes economic growth, but without excessive and unsustainable budgetary spending. the introduction of the euro is a major achievement for the republic of croatia and it is therefore a pleasure to support this report and to thank the rapporteur sincerely for his work.
mr president, thank you very much to your colleagues for your support, but also for recognising the enormous progress, the enormous efforts of the croatian government in meeting all the demanding criteria for the introduction of the euro. i must also answer, mr ilčić, because it is obvious that some people today, unfortunately, forget that the kuna was never an end in itself, but a tool for a better quality of life for croatian families. if we can do this better, more strongly with the euro today, and there is no question about that, i see no reason why we cannot. currency is always a tool of an economy, and can never be just its ornament. croats still save in euros, exporting and importing companies do business in euros, and because of the euro, we already enjoy a record credit rating. with the euro, but also with the record european resources at our disposal that encourage entrepreneurship and innovation, digitalisation is a truly first-class generational opportunity that we must seize with strength, ambition, attractiveness and openness. because we can and because the croatian people deserve it!
dear chairman, thank you very much. as part of its 'field to table' strategy, the european commission announced that it will present new rules on front-of-pack labelling this year. it is clear that this will enable consumers to obtain the most important information on the nutritional value of the product in a clear and fair manner. now, this information is printed in small letters on the back of the pack, which is why i often hear consumers complaining that they have difficulty finding it. however, i would like to warn the commission from this place that food labels can be easily manipulated. for example, the nutri-score label ranks a carbonated beverage with artificial sweetener, such as coca-cola, as more desirable in terms of nutritional value than virgin olive oil, which is highly unacceptable. i therefore call on the commission once again to take account of the specific characteristics of olive oil when drawing up its proposal for labelling and to prevent consumer manipulation. we must work together to preserve the mediterranean diet, of which olive oil is an essential ingredient.

member of the commission. – first of all, there is no chance that we can succeed in the fight against climate change or halting biodiversity loss without the oceans playing a vital role, oceans, of course, including the sea basins that we all know best. and here, i think, speaking against the background of the ocean conference in lisbon, we can clearly say that this year we need to use, as much as is possible, to actually put the ocean nexus into the final documents of cop15. it must be also a clear visibility at cop in sharm el-sheikh, too, because without healthy oceans, without their mitigation functions, without absorption of co2, we won’t be able to fight climate change. and you are absolutely right. climate change is already putting huge pressure on our oceans and we see this with very quickly changing ecosystems, biodiversity, within our oceans. another thing which i think is going to be an important step on the way to montreal is negotiations on so-called bbnj – beyond national jurisdictions – on the protection of the high seas. that can give a great booster towards the meeting in montreal in finding an agreement on protection of the high seas and having clear rules there. so i hope that we will be able in august to have a final meeting in new york to close these negotiations. that would be a big step further as regards our oceans’ protection. the final thing is, of course, we also have to look and protect marine resources better in our seas. i think what’s very important is to step up the implementation of marine protected areas. so far we’ve seen significant steps done by some member states and we just had a huge announcement of the largest marine protected area, but still, it’s not enough. we need to do more. we need to be aiming at and reaching our 30% goal that we have in our biodiversity strategy. and of course, the member states will have to step up their efforts.
